The M8.2 sync streams were corrected to the supported PowerSync sync-rules SQL dialect (`edition: 3`): parameter (`with`) queries are self-contained and never reference another CTE, event-scoped authorization correlation uses `INNER JOIN`s inside data queries, and no `EXISTS` is used; the streams were validated by running the PowerSync service against a PostgreSQL source (the `deploy/docker` database service, which provisions the required replication role, bucket-storage database, and publication) with no fatal sync-config or `json_each` parameter-query errors. M16.15 binds the department self-administration side of the same section: the Admin surface and the team edit form now read `GET /api/departments/{department}/teams` and `GET /api/departments/{department}/teams/{team}` and write through the `update-department-details`, `create-team`, `update-team`, `archive-team`, `restore-team`, `assign-staff-to-team`, `remove-staff-from-team`, `select-team-lead`, and `remove-team-lead` commands, so the default-team, archive, lead-designation, and membership rules M11.13 and M11.17 enforce are the ones the screens obey. | Technical spec: Section 10.2 Event authority; Data/API spec: Section 7.4 Node Sync (event authority rules) | In progress | [M4.2](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M12.6](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M12.7](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `EventSchemaTest`; `EventAuthorityTest`; `GovernanceEditFreezeTest` | [QA-SYNC-01](../qa/QA-SYNC-01-onsite-central-sync.md) | M4.2 stored the active event window start/end fields on events. M12.6 enforces them. `EventAuthority` resolves an event's phase — preparation before the window, active inside it, closed after it, with an archived event and an event with no window start never active and an open-ended window staying active — and resolves the on-site primary node for an event as an active, paired `onsite` node whose `event_id` names that event or names nothing, preferring the one that names it. During the active window that node is authoritative and both write paths defer to it. `EventScopedWriteGuard` refuses local writes to event-scoped records on any node that does not hold authority, listening on Eloquent model events rather than per call site so a write path cannot forget to ask; records that reach their event through a parent (incident timeline entries, incident/Field Report links, incident responders, Field Report appends, shift assignments) are covered through that parent, and event-scoped `team_grants` writes are how the permission-change rule is enforced. The sync log, audit trail, incident list presets, and the `events` row itself are exempt, because a read-only node must still record what it received and refused and must still be able to close or extend the window that makes it read-only. `NodeOperationReceiver` refuses an authentic event-scoped operation whose origin node does not hold authority for that event, audited as `node_operation.rejected` with reason `event_authority_refused`, and stands the local write guard down while applying, because data arriving from the on-site primary node is the documented exception to a read-only node. Refused local writes surface as HTTP 409 with `reason_code`, `event_id`, and `authoritative_node_id`. Nothing is refused outside the active window, and nothing is refused when no on-site node is known, because authority is a handover and there would be no node to hand it to. M12.7 adds `GovernanceWriteGuard`, which freezes policy/procedure and fragment edits for the duration of the window on every node, including the on-site node that holds authority, because a fragment edit raises the version of every published document that references it and staff acknowledge a document at a version. Governance content carries no `event_id`, so the window is resolved through `organization_id` by `EventAuthority::activeEventForOrganization()`, and fragment references and fragment version bumps reach their organization through their fragment; an organization's window freezes only that organization's content. `updating` is listened to alongside `saving` and `deleting`, because `Model::increment()` fires only the former and that is the path the queued fragment revision bump takes. Acknowledgments, their version snapshots, and acknowledgment requirements stay writable, since acknowledgments collected on-site sync back to central and a requirement is the live signup gate rather than document content. Refused edits surface as HTTP 409 with reason code `governance_frozen_during_active_event` and no authoritative node, and applying a received operation stands the freeze down because no node can create a document operation while the window is open. The conflict queue (M12.8) is present; the resolver (M12.9), Electron surfacing (M12.10), post-close on-site write restrictions, and mass query-builder writes that bypass Eloquent events remain outside these tasks. |
| Technical spec: Section 10.1 Sync model; Section 10.4 Node operation schema; Data/API spec: Section 13.3 `node_operations`; Section 13.6 Node sync exchange endpoint | In progress | [M12.2](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M12.3](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M12.4](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M12.5](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `NodeOperationSchemaTest`; `NodeOperationSigningTest`; `NodeSignatureAlgorithmTest`; `NodeOperationReceiveApplyTest`; `NodeSyncLoopTest` | [QA-SYNC-01](../qa/QA-SYNC-01-onsite-central-sync.md) | `node_operations` now stores the full section 10.4 field set as append-only rows: operations cannot be deleted, and the normalized operation fields plus `payload_json`, `signature`, and `hash` cannot be rewritten after they are recorded, while only the documented delivery lifecycle columns (`sent_at`, `received_at`, `applied_at`, `status`, `failure_reason`, `retry_count`) may change. `uuid` is unique so repeated receipt of the same operation is safe, `created_at` carries the origin node's creation time with no `updated_at`, and the model exposes the normalized-field projection that operation signatures cover plus the `pending`/`sent`/`received`/`applied`/`failed`/`conflicted` statuses. M12.3 adds signing and verification over that projection: the canonical signed payload is the `meridian.node-operation.v1` format marker plus the normalized fields as ordered JSON, `hash` is the SHA-256 digest of that payload and is recomputed rather than trusted, and `signature` is the base64 detached node signature (Ed25519 or RSA/SHA-256, derived from the node key material that setup generates). Signing uses the node private key resolved from node config, refuses a peer node record or an already-recorded operation, pins the origin `created_at` before it is covered, and fills the origin node. Verification fails closed for a blank signature, a rewritten normalized field or hash, missing/unusable key material, an unknown origin node, or a signature from another node. Device-originated operations are countersigned by the accepting node only after the device signature verifies against the registered device public key and matches the operation's acting device, and `node_operation.signed`/`node_operation.countersigned` audit events retain both signatures in `signature_metadata_json`. M12.4 adds the receiving half of the sync path: `NodeOperationEnvelope` is the wire shape (normalized fields, `signature`, `hash`, `payload_json`, and no delivery lifecycle columns, because each node tracks its own send/receive/apply state), `NodeOperationReceiver::receive()` commits the operation as `received` in its own transaction before `apply()` runs, and `receiveAndApply()` keeps that order. Idempotency rests on `uuid`: a redelivery resolves to the stored operation before any acceptance check re-runs, is neither stored nor applied twice, and a concurrent duplicate insert resolves to the winner through the unique index; the same `uuid` with different content is refused as a replay. Refusal and failure stay distinct — a malformed envelope, an operation addressed to another node, an unknown or revoked origin node, an unknown acting user, an unknown or revoked acting device, and a signature that does not verify are refused without ever being stored and are audited as `node_operation.rejected` with a stable reason code, while an accepted operation that cannot be applied is stored and marked `failed` with a `failure_reason` and incremented `retry_count` so it stays recoverable and does not block unrelated sync. Application dispatches through `NodeOperationApplierRegistry` on `entity_type`/`operation_type`, commits the entity write together with the `applied` mark, and marks an operation no applier claims as `failed` rather than discarding it. Nothing is applied from `payload_json`: signatures cover the normalized fields only, so the payload is unauthenticated and changeable in transit, and appliers are handed the `SignedNodeOperation` projection with no access to it. The payload is still stored for conflict review but is non-authoritative, which means an operation's meaning must live in `operation_type` and the entity it names rather than in payload data. Per-entity appliers, the bidirectional sync loop (M12.5), event authority enforcement (M12.6, M12.7), and the conflict queue/resolver (M12.8, M12.9) remain with their owning tasks; M12.4 decides only that an operation is well formed, addressed here, authentic, and from a node and device this install still accepts. M12.5 closes the loop with the sending half and the transport. `NodeOperationRecorder` creates an operation on this node, signs it before insert, and stores it `pending`, which is the queue on-site holds operations in while there is no internet; `NodeSyncOutbox` decides what is still owed to a peer, offering only operations that originated here so two nodes cannot bounce the same operation back and forth, oldest first so ordered entities arrive in origin order, and bounded by a batch size. `POST /api/node-sync` is one exchange in both directions: the caller pushes its queued operations in the request and this node's queued operations come back in the response, which is what makes sync bidirectional rather than push-only without central having to reach into an event network. The exchange is signed by the calling node over `meridian.node-sync.v1` plus the source node, `sent_at`, and the operation/acknowledgement/refusal uuid lists, and verified against the peer public key registered at pairing; operation contents are not covered because each operation is separately node-signed. Exchange authentication exists because the response hands operations back, so an unauthenticated caller could otherwise pull this node's queue. An exchange from an unknown, revoked, or never-paired node, one outside a two-sided clock window that bounds replay, and one whose signature does not verify are refused with a stable reason code and audited as `node_sync.refused`, and nothing is returned. Delivery state is confirmation-based: `pending` becomes `sent` only when the peer says it holds the operation, because a response lost in flight would otherwise drop an operation nobody would resend, while redelivery is safe on `uuid`. An operation the peer refuses becomes `failed` with the peer's reason so it leaves the queue instead of being offered forever, and a peer can only settle operations that originated on the receiving node. One refused operation is reported and the rest of the batch continues (technical spec 10.3). A run is a loop of exchanges that carries the previous exchange's acknowledgements and refusals forward, so a backlog drains within a run and no ack bookkeeping is persisted between runs; an interrupted run just gets the operations re-offered and stores them idempotently. `meridian:node-sync` runs the loop and is scheduled every minute, exiting successfully on an unreachable peer because a queued on-site node is the designed behavior, and failing when the node is unpaired, cannot sign, or is refused. Event authority during an active event window (M12.6, M12.7), the conflict queue and resolver (M12.8, M12.9), and Electron sync health (M12.10) remain with their owning tasks; per-entity operation vocabularies and appliers stay with the tasks that own each entity's sync. M12.5 also adds the God-mode read of that state: `NodeSyncHealth` derives sync health from the operation log and the refusal audit trail rather than storing a run status, because a stored status would let a run that died mid-way look healthier than one that finished and reported a problem. The node configuration screen shows queued/delivered/refused in the sending direction and received/applied/unapplied in the receiving direction — counted apart because undelivered means this node cannot reach its peer while unapplied means the peer was reached and something local is wrong — plus the oldest queued operation, last send, last receipt, recent operation failures, and recent refused exchanges, which have no operation row and exist only in audit. Queued work is reported without alarm because a backlog during an outage is the designed behavior; only failures and refusals set the attention state. Electron health (M12.10) surfaces the same signals on-site. |

| Technical spec: Section 11.1 Authentication providers | In progress | [M3.2](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M3.3](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M3.4](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.3](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `MagicLinkAuthTest`; `GoogleOAuthAuthTest`; `DiscordOAuthAuthTest`; `ApiProviderHandoffTest`; pending secondary email/provider-linking tests | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md), [QA-AUTH-02](../qa/QA-AUTH-02-google-oauth-login.md), [QA-AUTH-03](../qa/QA-AUTH-03-discord-oauth-login.md) | Email magic-link login path implemented for central authentication with configurable first-time account creation defaulting on for Alpha 1 testing (M3.2); Google OAuth login path implemented with verified-email enforcement and provider linking by primary email (M3.3); Discord OAuth implemented with verified-email enforcement and provider linking by primary email (M3.4). No internal password login was added. M16.3 gives both providers a second entry point for client applications: the same node-side provider exchange, reached through a system-browser handoff, so a client application signs a user in with Google or Discord without the provider flow being reimplemented in the client and without a browser session being left open (`OAuthProviderGateway` is the shared abstraction both provider services now implement). Secondary email matching and explicit secondary-email provider linking remain pending. |
| AUTH-018 | Complete | [M16.1](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.11](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `ApiTokenIssuanceTest`; `ApiTokenExpiryTest`; `FieldReportCommandHttpTest`; `SharedWorkstationSessionTest`; `apps/client/src/session/apiLogin.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/session/apiToken.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/session/deviceIdentity.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/views/LoginViews.spec.ts` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | Meridian client applications can now authenticate to the API with a Sanctum bearer token instead of a browser session cookie: `laravel/sanctum` is added to the approved baseline, `users` carry API tokens, and `config/sanctum.php` disables the stateful-SPA cookie mode and empties Sanctum's fallback guard list so a console browser session cannot authenticate an `auth:sanctum` route. M16.11 finishes it: the `local.field` shared-token middleware, its configuration, and the client environment variable that carried its token are removed, every operational route moved behind `auth:sanctum,workstation`, and the shared Vue client — which is the mobile Field application — signs in for itself. It asks for a login code, exchanges it with the device identity it generates and keeps, holds the issued token durably under `meridian.api-token.v1`, sends it on every request, disposes of it on sign-out, and drops it when the node refuses it. The seeded fixture user survives as ordinary development seed data and is reached by signing in as that user. Revocation is also visible on the device it affects: the client re-resolves its session when somebody returns to the application, so an operator revoking a token in God Mode signs that device out without waiting for a restart, and a client that loses its session moves to the sign-in surface instead of leaving somebody on a screen they no longer hold. Neither is an enforcement boundary — the node refuses the credential at request time regardless (AUTH-023). |
| AUTH-019 | Complete | [M16.1](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `ApiTokenIssuanceTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | `POST /api/auth/magic-link` mails a single-use login code for an email address, and `POST /api/auth/magic-link/verify` exchanges that code for a bearer token, so a client completes verification without leaving the application. The request endpoint answers identically for known and unknown addresses so it cannot enumerate accounts; codes are stored only as a keyed hash, are single use, expire, retire after a bounded number of wrong guesses, and both routes are rate limited. `DELETE /api/auth/session` lets a client revoke the token it holds. Provider handoff through a system browser is AUTH-020, delivered in M16.3. |
| AUTH-020 | Complete | [M16.3](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `ApiProviderHandoffTest` | [QA-AUTH-02](../qa/QA-AUTH-02-google-oauth-login.md), [QA-AUTH-03](../qa/QA-AUTH-03-discord-oauth-login.md) | Google and Discord login from a client application completes in a system browser and returns the result to the requesting application, and the provider exchange stays on the node. `GET /api/auth/{provider}/start` is called by the client — not opened in the browser — so an unconfigured provider or an unoffered client target is a refusal the application can explain rather than an error page in a browser it cannot see; it answers with the provider authorization URL, the return address, and the handoff state. The provider returns the browser to the node's existing `login/{provider}/callback`, which recognizes a handoff state and, instead of opening a session there, resolves the identity and sends the browser on to the client's registered return address. `POST /api/auth/session` exchanges the returned code for a device-bound bearer token. What crosses the return leg is a one-time exchange code rather than the token itself, and redemption requires the PKCE verifier (RFC 7636) for the challenge the handoff started with, because on mobile and desktop that leg travels through a custom scheme another application on the machine can register; possession of the code alone is therefore insufficient, and a refused verifier does not spend the code, so an interception cannot cancel the real client's sign-in. Return addresses are resolved from node configuration per client target — `MERIDIAN_API_HANDOFF_RETURN_WEB`, `_MOBILE`, and `_DESKTOP` — never from the request, so the handoff cannot become an open redirect, and blanking one disables provider login for that client target. Handoff state values and exchange codes are stored only as keyed hashes; the whole round trip is bounded by `MERIDIAN_API_HANDOFF_EXPIRES_MINUTES`, default 10. Failures return to the client target carrying a reason (`provider_denied`, `unverified_email`, `account_disabled`, `handoff_expired`, `callback_incomplete`, `provider_failed`, `handoff_already_answered`) because the application is the only surface that can explain them. A handoff establishes no browser session, and the provider callbacks moved out of the `guest` group so a browser already holding a Meridian session is not bounced away from a sign-in a client is waiting on. Token issuance from this path is audited with reason `provider_handoff`. |
| AUTH-024 | Complete | [M16.1](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `ApiTokenExpiryTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | Bearer tokens expire on a node-configured lifetime, `MERIDIAN_API_TOKEN_EXPIRATION_MINUTES`, catalogued in `.env.example` and therefore overridable from God Mode system configuration. The documented default is 60480 minutes — six weeks — matching the trusted-session window in technical spec 11.2, because users authenticate before an event while internet exists and the event may then run for days without connectivity. Issuance stamps each token's own `expires_at` and the guard also enforces the configured lifetime, so lowering the setting expires tokens already issued at their next request. An unusable setting falls back to the documented default rather than issuing a token that never expires. Expiry is independent of the 5-minute shared-workstation inactivity timeout in technical spec 13.3. |
| AUTH-021 | Complete | [M16.2](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `ApiTokenDeviceBindingTest`; `ApiTokenIssuanceTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | Every issued bearer token is bound to a `devices` record. The verify request carries a `device` object — a stable install identifier the client generates once, plus the label, platform, and device public key the node needs the first time it sees that identifier — and `personal_access_tokens.device_id` is `NOT NULL`, so an unbound token is not representable rather than merely not issued. A request naming no device, a malformed identifier, an unknown device sending no registration details, key material the node cannot verify with the same algorithm that checks device signatures under technical spec 12.4, or a revoked device is refused with a `device_unresolvable` or `device_revoked` reason and no token. Resolution is two-phase around the code redemption: the device is checked before the code is spent, so a correctable device refusal does not cost the person their single-use code, and the `devices` row is written only after the code proves good, so the sessionless login endpoint cannot be used to register hardware that never signed in. There is no separate device registration endpoint in Alpha 1; first sight of an identifier is where a `devices` row begins, which is what `first_seen_at` records. |
| AUTH-022 | Complete | [M16.2](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `ApiTokenRevocationTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | The God Mode console screen `orchid.api-tokens` (route `platform.api-tokens`, permission `platform.api-tokens`) lists issued tokens with their user, bound device, status, issue time, last use, and expiry, narrowed by user and by device. It revokes one token, and revokes every token bound to a device once the list is narrowed to that device — offered only there, so the decision is made while looking at exactly the tokens it ends. Both actions re-check the permission on the method request, because a screen method is its own request. The token value is never displayed and cannot be: only its SHA-256 hash is stored. Expired and revoked tokens stay listed, because an operator asking what could reach this node from a device is asking about credentials that existed. |
| AUTH-023 | Complete | [M16.2](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `ApiTokenRevocationTest`; `ApiTokenIssuanceTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | Revocation stamps `personal_access_tokens.revoked_at` and is evaluated on the node at request time through `Sanctum::authenticateAccessTokensUsing`, so a revoked token stops authenticating on its next request without the client cooperating and without the device being reachable when the decision is made. The same request-time check refuses a token whose bound device is revoked (data/API 12.5, "revoking a device revokes its tokens") and one whose device row has gone missing. A client signing itself out through `DELETE /api/auth/session` now stamps the same column rather than deleting the row, so God Mode can still account for the credential and the audit entries naming it keep resolving. |
| AUTH-025 | Complete | [M16.2](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `ApiTokenAuditTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | Issuance, revocation, and expiry are audited as `api_token.issued`, `api_token.revoked`, and `api_token.expired`, each naming the token by identifier and the bound device by identifier, with the revocation reason (`client_signed_out`, `god_mode_token`, `god_mode_device`) recorded on the entry. Issuance is audited in the same transaction that writes the token. Revoking a device's tokens audits each token in its own right rather than one entry naming a count. Expiry is the one audited moment nobody performs, so it is recorded when the node first observes it — the next request the expired token makes — and once only, so a client retrying an expired token does not accumulate entries. No raw token value or stored hash reaches an audit entry, a log, or the console; the plaintext exists in the issuing HTTP response and nowhere else. |
| AUTH-026 | Complete | [M16.8](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeTest`; `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeOrchidTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | Shared-workstation login codes are generatable both ways. God mode generates one for any known user on the console screen `orchid.shared-workstation-login-codes` (route `platform.shared-workstation-login-codes`, permission `platform.shared-workstation-login-codes`), which is the assisted-recovery and event-preparation path. A user generates one for themselves at `POST /api/auth/shared-workstation-login-code`, behind `auth:sanctum`, because holding a session on the calling device is the whole authority for the request — and every issued token is device-bound (AUTH-021), so the generation audit entry names which of a person's devices did it. Both paths run through one domain method and produce the same kind of code: `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeService::generate()` applies the trusted-workstation rule, the pinned-event scope, the rate limits, and the audit entry regardless of which authority called it, so the two cannot drift apart. Codes are eight characters from an alphabet that excludes the symbols people transcribe wrongly (`I`, `L`, `O`, `U`, `0`, `1`), shared with the mailed API login code through `App\Support\TypableCode` because both are typed by hand. |
| AUTH-027 | Complete | [M16.8](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeTest`; `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeOrchidTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | Self-service generation reaches nothing but the local database. There is no mail, no central call, and no other out-of-band channel on the path: a test drives the endpoint with `Http::preventStrayRequests()` and `Mail::fake()`, with no central node URL configured, and the code is still issued. This is the reason the mechanism exists — it is the only login path that survives an on-site node with no route to central, so anything it depended on would be the thing that broke during an event. |
| AUTH-028 | Complete | [M16.8](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeTest`; `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeOrchidTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | A self-service code is scoped to the generating user, and the scope is enforced twice over. The endpoint carries no user field, so there is structurally nothing to put another person's identifier in; a request that names one anyway is refused with `self_service_scope` rather than quietly reinterpreted, because a client that believes it can do this should be told it cannot. `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeService::generate()` refuses the same thing independently of any caller, so a future caller cannot reintroduce it. `generated_by_user_id` records who generated each code, which is also how the console distinguishes the two authorities without storing a third thing: a code whose generator is its own user was self-generated. God mode retains generation for another user, gated on `platform.shared-workstation-login-codes` and re-checked on the screen method because a screen method is its own request. |
| AUTH-029 | Complete | [M16.8](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeTest`; `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeOrchidTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | Three limits, bounding three different things. Generation is limited per user and per node through `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeThrottle`: the per-user counter is keyed to the user a code is *for*, which bounds how many live codes one person can hold and is the limit a stolen session runs into, while keying it to the operator would have made God mode's documented event-preparation path collide with a limit meant for a different problem; the per-node counter bounds the whole install and defaults well above it so preparing a roster stays inside it. Code entry is limited per workstation, because guessing happens at the kiosk keyboard rather than at generation; every attempt is counted, not only the failures, and a successful sign-in clears the counter so a workstation in normal use is never throttled by its own traffic. Failed entries are audited only once a workstation has produced `failed_entry_audit_threshold` of them in a window (technical spec 13.2, "audited after a threshold"), so one mistyped character is not an audit entry. All four values are `.env.example`-catalogued and therefore adjustable from God Mode system configuration; an unusable value falls back to its documented default rather than removing a limit. The route also carries a request throttle, which answers a different question — how hard one caller may ask — before any state is read. |
| AUTH-030 | Complete | [M16.9](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `SharedWorkstationSessionTest`; `apps/client/src/session/workstationSession.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/views/KioskSessionViews.spec.ts` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | Entering a login code establishes a `shared_workstation_sessions` row and nothing else. The negative half is the requirement, so it is asserted directly: one test enters a valid code and then counts `personal_access_tokens` and `device_trusts`, finding neither, and reads the response for any token-shaped key; the client spec asserts the same from the other side, that no request the Kiosk makes carries an `Authorization` header. The session is a separate credential from a token rather than a token with a flag — its own table, its own `X-Meridian-Workstation-Session` header, and its own `workstation` guard registered through `Auth::viaRequest` — because AUTH-024 makes the two lifetimes independent and a shared column is how something eventually treats one as the other. `/api/me` accepts both guards, so the active user's roles and capabilities resolve through one endpoint and there is no second answer to what a kiosk user may do. The session is not authority of its own: it resolves to a user, and a user disabled after signing in resolves to nobody. |
| Data/API spec: Section 12.5 API tokens | Complete | [M16.1](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.2](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `ApiTokenDeviceBindingTest`; `ApiTokenRevocationTest`; `ApiTokenAuditTest`; `ApiTokenExpiryTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md) | `personal_access_tokens` carries the documented fields: the polymorphic owner resolving to a user, `device_id`, `name`, the hashed `token`, `abilities`, `last_used_at`, `expires_at`, `revoked_at`, and `created_at`. `App\Models\ApiToken` replaces Sanctum's token model through `Sanctum::usePersonalAccessTokenModel` so the binding travels with every token the guard reads on an incoming request. The M16.2 migration recreates the table rather than altering it: SQLite cannot add a `NOT NULL` column, and a token issued before device binding cannot acquire a device retroactively, so it must not survive — the clients holding one sign in again. | Technical spec: Section 11.4 API tokens | Complete | [M16.1](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.2](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.3](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.11](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `ApiTokenIssuanceTest`; `ApiTokenExpiryTest`; `ApiTokenDeviceBindingTest`; `ApiTokenRevocationTest`; `ApiTokenAuditTest`; `ApiProviderHandoffTest`; `FieldReportCommandHttpTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md), [QA-AUTH-02](../qa/QA-AUTH-02-google-oauth-login.md), [QA-AUTH-03](../qa/QA-AUTH-03-discord-oauth-login.md) | The API magic-link issuance path and node-configured expiry are implemented (M16.1). M16.2 binds every issued token to a `devices` record, refuses issuance without a resolvable device, adds the God Mode `orchid.api-tokens` screen listing tokens by user and by device with revocation of one token or of every token on a device, evaluates revocation at request time, and audits issuance, expiry, and revocation without ever writing a raw token value. M16.3 adds the second issuance path, provider handoff, and amends this section: what the system browser carries back to the application is a one-time exchange code redeemed with a PKCE verifier rather than the token itself, because that leg travels through a custom scheme another application can register, and a handoff establishes no browser session. M16.11 removes the `local.field` shared-token middleware, its configuration, and the client environment variable, and moves every operational route behind `auth:sanctum,workstation` — the second guard because a Kiosk authenticates with a shared-workstation session key rather than a personal token (AUTH-030) and a workstation that could not record a check-in would not be one. The section now also states what a client does with the token it holds: keeps it across restarts, disposes of it on sign-out, and drops it when a refused session refresh says the node has withdrawn it. Meridian ships its own `personal_access_tokens` migration rather than Sanctum's because `users.id` is a UUID. |
| Data/API spec: Section 5.4 API authentication | Complete | [M16.1](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.2](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.3](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.8](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.9](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `ApiTokenIssuanceTest`; `ApiTokenExpiryTest`; `ApiTokenDeviceBindingTest`; `ApiTokenAuditTest`; `ApiProviderHandoffTest`; `SharedWorkstationLoginCodeTest`; `SharedWorkstationSessionTest` | [QA-AUTH-01](../qa/QA-AUTH-01-email-magic-link-login.md), [QA-AUTH-02](../qa/QA-AUTH-02-google-oauth-login.md), [QA-AUTH-03](../qa/QA-AUTH-03-discord-oauth-login.md) | `POST /api/auth/magic-link`, `POST /api/auth/magic-link/verify`, and `DELETE /api/auth/session` are implemented with node-configured expiry (M16.1). The verify request now carries the device the token is bound to, and a request that cannot supply a resolvable device identity is refused rather than issued an unbound token (M16.2). Issuance, expiry, and revocation are audited, and raw token values are never logged, audited, or exported (M16.2). `GET /api/auth/{provider}/start` and `POST /api/auth/session` complete the endpoint list with the provider handoff (M16.3), and section 5.4 now documents that contract: the client target and PKCE challenge going in, the exchange code and state coming back, and the rules that keep the return leg safe. Every rule listed in 5.4 is enforced on both issuance paths. M16.11 removes the `local.field` middleware this section says is removed, and adds the rule that was left implicit: every command and every client read requires a bearer token or a shared-workstation session key, and the only routes carrying no credential are node-to-node exchange, the login paths themselves, and the branding profile read a device resolves before it has a session. Neither the single-use `api_login_codes` table nor `api_auth_handoffs` is enumerated in data/API section 12. Both follow the `shared_workstation_login_codes` shape in 12.4 — keyed hash only, single use, bounded lifetime — because an API client completes verification in the application rather than in a browser, so what the node hands out has to be a value the client can carry back. M16.8 adds the one endpoint this section was missing on the shared-workstation side: `POST /api/auth/shared-workstation-login-code`, which is not an API credential path and issues no token, but is reached the same way — a signed-in client asking the node for a code its own user will type at a kiosk. It is documented here rather than in section 12 because what needed specifying was the contract, not the table: the request carries only the workstation, the response carries the code once, the event comes from the workstation's pinned context, and there is no user field to name somebody else with. M16.9 adds the three endpoints a shared-workstation session is started, read, and ended through, documented here for the same reason: the table is section 12.6 and the contract is the part clients build against. They are the one authenticated path in this section that carries no bearer token — the session key travels in `X-Meridian-Workstation-Session` under its own guard, deliberately not in `Authorization`, so nothing on either side can treat a workstation session as a personal one. |

| CLIENT-008 | Complete | [M16.5](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.23](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `apps/client/src/session/sessionStaleness.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/session/clientSession.spec.ts` | [QA-CLIENT-01](../qa/QA-CLIENT-01-session-and-api-wiring.md) | A cached document grants access only while the window of the event its context resolved to is open. The bound is the event's `active_event_window_ends_at` — the same window that decides event authority, so the cache expires when the event's operational phase closes rather than at a separate client-side deadline — falling back to the event's own `ends_at` for an event that never declared an operational phase. A window with no recorded end has not ended, following the event-authority rule verbatim, so an open-ended event does not strand a device mid-operation. Once the window has passed, or the document carries no event context, or its context names an event the document does not carry (which a node locked to an event produces for a caller holding no association with it), the client refuses access until a refresh succeeds: `sessionCapabilities()` returns nothing, so a surface that forgets to check the status still finds no capability to render against. The verdict is re-decided on every refresh attempt rather than only at boot, because a window ending underneath a running application is how a long offline stretch ends. A document straight from the node is always usable — the bound is on the cached copy, which is the copy that can outlive the answer it came from. |

Cached state is stated as information and expired state as a warning, per UI operating guide 18.2A: a device operating normally offline inside its event window is not in a failure state, and dressing it as one trains the indicator out of a user's attention. It renders nothing for a client holding no session at all, which is a sign-in state rather than a permission state. It lives on the Settings surface rather than in the application shell, alongside device readiness and node health, and reports the current state there as well as the stale ones — a page opened to ask this question should answer it, and a section that goes quiet whenever the answer is "fine" reads as broken at the moment it is working. It is still deliberately separate from the OfflineBanner, which keeps its shell placement: a device can be online with stale permissions or offline with fresh ones, connectivity changes what the next action will do, and permission state is a standing property of the session (UI implementation contract 19A.2). The shell placement was revised because a persistent banner during ordinary offline operation is the state a field device spends most of an event in; the accepted cost is that a user who cannot find a control opens Settings to learn why. |

| Technical spec: Section 11A.3 Context resolution; UI implementation contract Section 19A.3 Context switching; UI operating guide Section 8.3A | Complete | [M16.4](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.7](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `SessionResolutionTest`; `apps/client/src/session/sessionContext.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/views/ContextViews.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/components/AppShell.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/branding/brandingContext.spec.ts` |  | `SessionResolver` answers the context half — the lock decides, a single association decides where there is no lock, a requested event must be one of the caller's own, and `switching_available` is false on a node locked to an event because that node holds one event's records and no others. The client half is `apps/client/src/session/sessionContext.ts`, which reads that answer and adds no rule of its own beyond the two the client is the only one able to apply: whether this device can currently reach the node, and what has to be dropped when the context moves. | CLIENT-015 | Complete | [M16.10](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.21](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.23](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `apps/client/src/outbox/commandOutbox.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/submitCommand.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/syncCommandOutbox.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/CommandOutboxNotice.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/field-reports/submitFieldReport.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/session/workstationSession.spec.ts` | [QA-CLIENT-01](../qa/QA-CLIENT-01-session-and-api-wiring.md) | Every command a surface issues goes through one durable local queue. `CommandOutbox` holds each command as the request body it will send, keyed by the idempotency key the device generated, and `commandOutboxLocalStore` persists it, so a command survives the tab being reloaded, the Electron process restarting, and a shared-workstation session ending — the queue is deliberately absent from the context reset registry that a session end and an event switch run, because queued work is not context data (technical spec 13.3). The two Alpha 1 queues are gone: `PendingFieldReportQueue`, `PendingAttendanceQueue`, and the attendance operation store are deleted, and Field Reports and attendance now enqueue through `queueCommand` and drain through `syncCommandOutbox` as callers of the shared queue rather than owners of their own. A command that was on the wire when the process died is restored as queued rather than dropped or left in flight, because nothing on the device knows whether the node saw it and the key is what makes sending it again the same command. M16.21 gives attendance its first real caller: the Logistics Window queues check-in, check-out, and no-show through `queueCommand` rather than mutating a local model, so the desk keeps working with no node in reach and each operation applies once when one returns. The command bodies dropped `origin_node_id`, which the browser had no way to learn and which the fixture had been supplying; the node that receives the command records itself as the origin instead. |
| CLIENT-016 | Complete | [M16.10](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.23](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `apps/client/src/outbox/commandOutbox.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/submitCommand.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/syncCommandOutbox.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/CommandOutboxNotice.spec.ts`; `AttendanceCommandHttpTest`; `FieldReportAcceptanceTest` | [QA-CLIENT-01](../qa/QA-CLIENT-01-session-and-api-wiring.md) | The key is client-generated and is the device's own operation UUID: a Field Report's record id, an attendance operation's `operation_uuid`. The queue is addressed by it, so issuing the same command twice returns the entry already held rather than adding a second, and the held entry's attempt history and payload are never replaced by the repeat. A drain that runs three times over an accepted command puts one request on the wire; a drain interrupted mid-flight re-sends under the same key. The server half was already in place and is unchanged: `submit-field-report` and the three attendance commands treat a repeated key as the same command and produce no duplicate domain effect (data/API 5.3). |
| CLIENT-017 | Complete | [M16.10](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.23](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `apps/client/src/outbox/commandOutbox.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/submitCommand.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/syncCommandOutbox.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/CommandOutboxNotice.spec.ts` | [QA-CLIENT-01](../qa/QA-CLIENT-01-session-and-api-wiring.md) | Queued, sending, accepted, and rejected are the four states in UI implementation contract 16.3, used verbatim wherever they are reported. `CommandOutboxNotice` sits in the shell beside the OfflineBanner and the session permissions notice — not on the surface that issued the work, because the person who queued a check-in and walked away is the one the requirement is about — and states held work as information and refused work as a warning, listing each refusal with the node's reason and a dismiss control. Nothing dismisses a rejection on a user's behalf. Accepted commands are reported alongside outstanding work rather than on their own, so a device with nothing left to send shows no standing banner (contract 16.2), and the full queue including accepted commands is on the device diagnostics surface, where sync detail belongs (operating guide 17.4). Accepted history is bounded at 50 entries; queued and rejected commands are never pruned. A refusal is carried back to the record it belongs to, not only to the queue: the Field Report detail surface reads the command rather than the record’s own `pending_sync` flag, so a refused report reads "Refused by the node: <reason>" instead of "Queued locally" beside a "Retry sync" that drains a queue the report is deliberately not in. Because a refusal is not always the last word — a misconfigured node, a shift not yet started — the person shown the reason can put the command back in the queue under its original key, from the notice or from the report. Nothing retries on their behalf, which is the half of the requirement that keeps a refusal visible. The drain draws the line the requirement depends on: only the node deciding against a command makes it rejected, while an unreachable node, a 5xx, a throttle, and an expired credential return it to the queue, because losing a recorded check-in to a lapsed token is not a refusal of the work. |
| CLIENT-018 | Complete | [M16.10](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.20](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.21](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M16.23](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `apps/client/src/outbox/commandOutbox.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/submitCommand.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/syncCommandOutbox.spec.ts`; `apps/client/src/outbox/CommandOutboxNotice.spec.ts` | [QA-CLIENT-01](../qa/QA-CLIENT-01-session-and-api-wiring.md) | The command catalog decides, not the caller. Data/API 7.2 closes the list of Alpha 1 offline writes and 5.6 says so, so the catalog is default-deny: a command is connected-only unless it is registered as one of the four offline writes. `queueCommand` refuses a connected-only command outright and never writes it to the queue; `sendConnectedCommand` refuses one where there is no connection, before anything reaches the network. Each refusal carries the catalog's own reason in the words the person who issued it is shown. The four families technical spec 11A.5 names by hand — incident creation, policy and procedure acknowledgment, event application submission, and map editing — are registered now, ahead of the surfaces that will issue them in M16.19 through M16.21, so the rule was under test before there was a screen that could break it. M16.20 registered the rest of the incident family and M16.21 the rest of the department operations desk: presence, unscheduled shift addition, deployment moves, and equipment handoff are connected-only, while the attendance beside them stays queued. The line between them is not arbitrary — data/API 7.2 lists check-in, check-out, and no-show, and each of those is recorded against a shift the device already holds, whereas presence turns on every shift and checkout in the department, an addition on trainings and waivers, and a handoff on whether an item is still where the node last saw it. None of those are questions a device can answer for itself, so each is sent now or refused now rather than queued against an answer that may already be wrong. M18.4 registers `correct-hours` on the same side of the line and it is the case that shows the line is about the question rather than the family: a correction writes an attendance operation exactly as its queued siblings do, and still cannot be held, because whether it is allowed at all is measured against a grace period the node's clock owns and closes on its own. One queued on a device may be delivered into a window that has already shut, after the operator walked away believing the total was fixed. |

`EventInfoHttpTest` | [QA-ORG-02](../qa/QA-ORG-02-organizer-department-admin.md), [QA-ORG-03](../qa/QA-ORG-03-organizer-staff-intake.md), [QA-TEAM-02](../qa/QA-TEAM-02-department-self-admin.md), [QA-TRAIN-01](../qa/QA-TRAIN-01-training-management.md), [QA-EQUIP-01](../qa/QA-EQUIP-01-equipment-inventory-setup.md), [QA-TEAM-03](../qa/QA-TEAM-03-team-shift-administration.md), [QA-POL-02](../qa/QA-POL-02-product-document-authoring-sharing.md), [QA-STAFF-02](../qa/QA-STAFF-02-staff-me-and-event-info.md); [QA-CLIENT-01](../qa/QA-CLIENT-01-session-and-api-wiring.md) | M16.14 binds the first group. The organizer department list/create/edit surfaces and the organizer staff surface now read and write through `/api/organizations/{organization}/departments`, `/api/organizations/{organization}/staff`, and the `create-department` / `update-department` / `archive-department` / `restore-department` / `add-organization-staff` / `select-department-lead` / `remove-department-lead` commands. Their fixture models and the development-session installer behind them are gone, and with them the organization id that was compiled into the client: `organizerAdminSession` reads the organization off the selected department in the session document and checks `organization.departments.manage` / `organization.staff.manage` on the roles that document attached to that department, so someone who organizes one organization and is ordinary staff in another is refused while working in the second. Because the organization now comes from the department switcher, a context switch reloads the surface; the watch is keyed on the organization id rather than on the session object, which is rebuilt on every refresh (CLIENT-014). The status filter is a request parameter rather than a predicate over a local copy, and every write is followed by a re-read rather than a local patch, so what the surface shows is what the node holds. These are connected-only administration surfaces (data/API 7.2): an unreachable node produces a stated read failure with a retry path rather than an empty organization, and no write is queued. Refusals are shown as the server worded them — `meridianErrorMessage` prefers the `errors` map, so a duplicate department code names the `code` field instead of collapsing to "(and 1 more error)" — and the router guard that used to install a session before entry is removed, because the surface is now restricted by the capability the session document carries (CLIENT-005, CLIENT-006). M16.15 binds the second group, the department's own administration of itself. The Admin surface fills its department details form, team table, team staff table, and staff assignment controls from the single `GET /api/departments/{department}/teams` the node already answers with all of them, and the team edit form reads `GET /api/departments/{department}/teams/{team}`; writes go through `update-department-details`, `create-team`, `update-team`, `archive-team`, `restore-team`, `assign-staff-to-team`, `remove-staff-from-team`, `select-team-lead`, and `remove-team-lead`, each followed by a re-read. Authority is the `access` block on those reads rather than a role the client decided for itself, so a team lead who does not administer the department gets the narrowed page and the disabled form the node's own answer describes, and the same answer that shaped the form is the one the command enforces. The fixture that used to stand in for all of this is reduced to what the surfaces still awaiting their tasks read — team overview, shift administration, and department operations — and is removed with them by M18.9. The status filter is the one exception to M16.14's rule against predicates over a local copy: the request asks for every team and the table narrows the response it just received, which is a view of one answer rather than a second, older copy of it. M16.16 binds the training surfaces. The list, the staff training page, and the edit screen read `GET /api/departments/{department}/trainings` and `GET /api/departments/{department}/trainings/{training}`; signup, cancellation, prerequisite maintenance, completion recording, spreadsheet import, create, update, archive, and restore go through the matching `/api/commands/*` endpoints, each followed by a re-read. The fixture model behind all of it is gone, and with it the client's own copies of two judgements that were the node's to make: whether a training takes signups, now `requires_scheduled_attendance` on the response rather than a guess from the start time, and whether the reader has completed a prerequisite, now `viewer_completed` on each prerequisite (TRAIN-010). The department read gained the `access` block, the team-scope options, and the department roster it needed in order to be the one read the edit screen renders from, and it carries each only on the terms the caller holds it — an ordinary member's read has no roster at all, so a member who reaches an edit URL has nothing to fill a form with. A caller who may record completions but not manage the department, which is a team lead of the training's team (TRAIN-005), gets the roster and completion panels with the details form replaced by a statement that they are not editable, the same shape M16.15 gave the team edit page. Prerequisite editing is diffed against the prerequisites the save response itself returned rather than against what the form was opened with, so a prerequisite added elsewhere while the form was open is not silently removed. There is no status filter to reconcile here: archived trainings arrive marked on a manager's read and are absent from a member's, which is the node's decision either way. `TrainingReadController::show` now refuses a training in another department, and an archived training read by a member, with a stated `Training not found for this department.` rather than a bare 404, matching its `teams` and `shifts` siblings, so the detail page prints the node's sentence instead of inventing one. M16.17 binds the equipment inventory surface. The `department.equipment` page and the featureset embedded in it render from the single `GET /api/departments/{department}/equipment`, and `create-equipment-item`, `update-equipment-item`, `archive-equipment-item`, `restore-equipment-item`, and `import-equipment-inventory` are each followed by a re-read, so the CSV import result the maintainer reads and the rows below it come from the same answer. The fixture model is gone, and with it three judgements the client had been making for itself: which states setup may set, now `maintainable_statuses` on the response; what each state is called, now `status_label` per item and `status_labels` alongside, giving UI contract 9.6's canonical labels one source instead of a second copy in TypeScript; and whether the caller may manage the inventory at all, now the `access` block, with a non-manager meeting the endpoint's own 403 sentence rather than client-authored copy. The read also stopped answering the locked-row question with a boolean: `has_open_checkout` is replaced by the checkout itself, carrying the holder's name, when it went out, and the shift it went out on, because the refusals a maintainer meets on a checked-out item — no state change, no archive — are only actionable if the screen says who to ask, and the shift is what tells a shift-assigned checkout from an event-assigned one (EQUIP-009). The Active/Archived/All filter is the same exception M16.15 made: `archived_at` is on every item, so the table narrows the response it already holds rather than issuing a second, older read, and the endpoint's `?status=` parameter goes unused by the product client. This is a connected-only setup surface (data/API 7.2), so an unreachable node is stated with a retry path rather than shown as a department with no equipment, and no add or archive is queued. Equipment checkout and return are not bound here: the Logistics Window is the only surface that performs them, it has no read endpoint yet, and the staff it offers are fixture identifiers, so `checkout-equipment` and `return-equipment` are bound by M16.21 with attendance, where real staff arrive. M16.18 binds the shift surfaces. The `department.shifts` page, the featureset embedded in Planning, and the create/edit form read `GET /api/departments/{department}/shifts` and `GET /api/departments/{department}/shifts/{shift}`, and `create-shift`, `update-shift`, `cancel-shift`, and `restore-shift` are each followed by a re-read. The fixture schedule is gone, and with it the browser's second copy of every rule `ShiftAdminService` enforces — end after start, signup close after signup open, capacity never below the assignments already made, the schedule and eligible team locked once a shift has started, cancel and restore refused past that point — along with the compiled-in training and waiver option tables the requirement checkboxes were built from. Two judgements moved to the node and are carried on the read: whether a shift has started, now `has_started` rather than the browser's clock, so the inputs the form disables are the ones the node has locked; and whether this caller may manage a given shift, now `can_manage` per row rather than a comparison against a team list, so the actions a row offers are the ones the commands would accept. The eligible-team field offers the `teams` the read returned, which the endpoint narrowed to the active teams this caller may schedule for, and a shift already sitting on a team outside that list still renders with its own team named rather than with a blank select that would move it on save. Unlike its M16.15 and M16.17 siblings the status filter is a request parameter here rather than a view of one response: cancelled shifts are absent from a member's read entirely, so narrowing locally would have meant deciding in the browser what the node had already decided differently for a different caller. The endpoint gained that member read as part of this task — a department member with active team membership reads the active shifts their teams are eligible for (SHIFT-004), read-only and with no option lists — because permission-aware navigation offers Shifts on the Staff menu to exactly those people (M16.6), and until now it opened onto bundled fixture data. This is a connected-only surface (data/API 7.2): an unreachable node is a stated read failure with a retry path rather than a department with nothing scheduled, and no create, cancel, or restore is queued. The operational shift board surfaces are not bound here: Department Overview, the Planning Table's plan-versus-actual rows, and Team Overview's shift rows are attendance aggregates whose purpose-built read models (data/API 5.1) the node does not serve yet, so they are bound by M16.21 with attendance and the Logistics workspace. M16.19 binds the document surfaces. The library page, the featureset embedded in Admin, the authoring form, and Event Info render from `GET /api/organizations/{organization}/documents`, `GET /api/policy-documents/{id}`, `GET /api/procedure-documents/{id}`, `GET /api/document-fragments/{id}`, and `GET /api/events/{event}/info`; `create-`/`update-`/`publish-`/`archive-policy-document`, the procedure equivalents, and `create-`/`update-document-fragment` are each followed by a re-read. The fixture library is gone, and with it the browser's second copy of four things the node already owns: who may maintain which scope, now `access.scopes` on the read rather than a predicate over a fixture department's role flags; whether a given row may be edited, now `can_maintain` per document, so a published document a caller may read but not maintain offers no Edit, Publish, or Archive; the six Event Info section keys and their empty descriptions, now `event_info_sections` on the document read and `empty_description` on the Event Info read; and the Markdown renderer, now `rendered_html` from `DocumentRenderer`, with fragments resolved, raw HTML stripped, and unsafe links refused (POL-022, POL-034, POL-035) — the client's renderer resolved only the fragments it happened to hold and stripped nothing, so what a reader saw was never quite the published document. Two behaviours changed as a consequence rather than by choice. Publish and archive now ask for the reason their commands require and the audit snapshot records, instead of transitioning silently. And exports go through M16.12: the editor asks for a short-lived URL scoped to that document and format and navigates to it, replacing the `data:` URL the client assembled for itself, so the file carries the node's metadata, resolved fragment text, branding, and export audit entry. The read gained the two answers an authoring form needs before it has a document to read them off — `access.scopes` and `event_info_sections` — and `can_maintain` per document, because a reader and a maintainer both receive published documents and only the row itself can say which is looking at it. The state filter is a request parameter, as in M16.18: draft and archived documents are absent from a reader's response entirely, so narrowing locally would have meant deciding in the browser what the node had already decided differently for a different caller. These are connected-only surfaces (data/API 7.2): an unreachable node is a stated read failure with a retry path rather than an organization with nothing published or an event with no guidance, and no save, publish, or archive is queued. The remaining CLIENT-023 surfaces — IMS (M16.20), attendance and logistics (M16.21), and reporting exports (M16.22) — stay with their owning tasks, and the fixture modules those surfaces still import are removed by M18.9. M16.20 binds the IMS surfaces: the incident list reads `GET /api/events/{event}/incidents` with its search, filters, sort, and page as query parameters and renders the page the node answered rather than narrowing one locally; detail, notes, links, attachments, and saved list presets each go to their command followed by a re-read; and the IMS Field Report review list reads the new `GET /api/events/{event}/field-reports`. The IMS fixture is gone, and with it the browser's second copy of incident numbering, the timeline entries a field change produces, the same-event link rule, the free-text search, and the preset cap. M16.21 binds the department operations surfaces. Department Overview, the Logistics Window, the Operations Center, and the Planning Table each render from one read under `/api/events/{event}/departments/{department}/`, and every write goes to a command followed by a re-read — a check-in closes somebody's off-site option, moves them onto the on-shift roster, and changes an exception count, and none of that comes back in the command's response. The department operations fixture stops backing those four surfaces, and with it the browser's own copies of who may go off-site and why not, what a check-in does to a shift card, which equipment moves to whose hands, which shift somebody may be added to, and the whole plan-versus-actual arithmetic. Authority is the `access` block those reads carry, resolved from the roles the presence, attendance, equipment, and deployment commands enforce. Three things the node had to answer that it did not: the four reads themselves, `mark-staff-on-site` and `mark-staff-off-site` for `DepartmentPresenceService`, and `add-staff-to-shift` for `UnscheduledShiftAdditionService` — the last two domain services that had been enforcing their rules since M10.4 and M10.7 with no route able to reach them. Two controls did not survive. The Planning Table's shift drill-down listed individual staff on the one surface SLB-019 forbids identities on, and now shows that shift's own counts. The Operations Center's "Add Deployment" and the Logistics Window's "Add Staff" printed a note that they were scaffolded; with the fixture gone there is no command behind either, and an action that cannot act is one CLIENT-006 says not to render. The fixture module survives for the surfaces still awaiting their tasks — Home, Me, Team Overview, and the Field Report dictation directory — and is removed with them by M18.9. M16.22 binds the last group, the reporting export entry points. `organizer.credentials` exists as a product surface for the first time and runs the credential eligibility export through the M16.12 download URL path, stating the event, the scope rule the node applies, the excluded fields, and the columns before generating anything (REPORT-010, REPORT-014). It reads nothing: an export is a file the node generates, audits, and names, so the surface owns the entry point and not a second copy of the data. The entry is permitted by `reports.credential_eligibility.export` from the session response and is absent rather than disabled without it, and the export is refused where it stands when the device has no network rather than queued, because an export is not a command the outbox could replay. The other four Alpha 1 exports have endpoints but no `download-url` sibling and no surface; both arrive with M18.25 and M18.26, which extend the descriptor list rather than replacing it. What still reads from fixtures after this is Home, Me, Team Overview, and the Field Report dictation directory, which M18.9 removes with their surfaces. M18.8 finishes the department operations group by proving the claim rather than restating it: `departmentOpsFixtureIsolation.spec.ts` walks the import graph out of Department Overview, the Logistics Window, the Operations Center, and the Planning Table and fails on any fixture module reachable from any of them, transitively. A rendering test cannot catch this — a view that keeps a fixture as a fallback renders correctly against a stubbed node and shows the seed data only when a real read comes back empty — and both previous regressions here were two or three modules down rather than in the view. The check carries a negative control asserting it still finds the fixture `MeView` legitimately imports, so a walker that silently stopped resolving specifiers would fail rather than pass. M18.9 removes the fixture models themselves. Home, Me, and Team Overview were the three product surfaces still rendering them while signed in with real data, and the first two were the most visible pages in the client: Home took its heading and operations window from `department-ops/fixtures.ts`, so a signed-in staff member was greeted by "Local Field Event" on dates belonging to no event they hold, and Me named "Local Field Author" whoever opened it. Team Overview resolved its own authority from a compiled-in list of who leads what and refused a real department lead a team in their own department. All three read the session document and their endpoints now. `department-ops/fixtures.ts`, `fixtureDepartmentAccess.ts`, and `fixtureDepartmentSession.ts` are deleted, the router's development department-lead session with them, and the dictated Field Report staff picker reads the node's department index rather than four invented people. The guard widens from the four department operations views to the whole production graph walked from `main.ts` and `App.vue`, which is the repository check the task asks for. `localFieldFixture` and `localFieldSession` remain on a named allowlist: they are the development session installers, they render on no screen while somebody is signed in, and they are what the client's specs stand on to run with no server (CLIENT-024). Removing them means deriving `FieldSessionContext` from the session document and moving 35 spec files onto a session builder, and is the last of M18.9. |
| Technical spec: Section 7 Node Model | In progress | [M3.5](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M3.6](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M12.1](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md), [M12.5](meridian-alpha-1-development-plan.md) |  | `NodeSchemaTest`; `NodeSetupTest`; `NodeConfigSourceTest`; `NodePairingTest`; `NodeSyncLoopTest` | [QA-NODE-01](../qa/QA-NODE-01-first-run-node-setup.md) | Node records now support node name, role, public key, nullable organization/event binding placeholders, central URL, and revocation timestamp (M3.5); first-run web setup creates exactly one active node with role selection; setup generates node key material and stores database-backed config values for source display (M3.6). Orchid God Mode can edit the active node name, role, and central URL after first run while preserving event-mode safeguards. A central node now issues one-time pairing tokens, and an on-site/standalone node pairs with central by redeeming one over `POST /api/node-pairing`; central registers the peer node, the paired node stores the central identity as node config values, and changing the central node URL puts pairing back into recheck (M12.1). Append-only node operation storage arrives with M12.2, and M12.3 puts the configured `node_private_key` to work signing node operations while `nodes.public_key` verifies them (see the Section 10.1/10.4 row). M12.4 makes the peer `nodes` record the gate on receipt: an operation from an unknown or revoked origin node, or one addressed to a different node, is refused before it is stored. M12.5 makes node ids global: a node keeps one `nodes.id` across every install that knows it, because a node operation names its origin and target nodes by id inside the signed message and that id cannot be rewritten on arrival without breaking verification, so pairing now exchanges ids in both directions and refuses an id already held by a different node identity. M12.5 also makes the peer `nodes` record the gate on the exchange itself: only a node record that is not this install's own, not revoked, and paired may exchange operations, and pairing status is rechecked before a node syncs so a changed central URL stops sync rather than pushing event data to whatever now answers there. Event binding enforcement and active-event authority remain deferred to M12.6 onward. |

Client-side readiness checks probe secure context, Web Crypto encryption or signature/key-generation primitives, and persistent key storage, failing closed with human-readable reasons when local encryption (M8.3) or device signing (M8.4) is unavailable per technical spec 12.3/12.4/26.2. The client event-mode gate (M8.7) combines the local-encryption and device-signing probes into a single fail-closed decision: event mode is `ready` only when both capabilities are available, and each missing capability is reported as a blocker with its reason per technical spec 8.6/26.2 ("client event mode must fail closed if local encryption or device signing is unavailable"). M8.8 adds human QA coverage for encryption/signing readiness and client fail-closed evidence. M12.3 delivers the server half of technical spec 12.4 device signatures: the accepting node verifies a device-originated operation's signature against the registered `device_public_key` over the operation's canonical payload, refuses to countersign when that signature does not verify or names a different acting device, countersigns with the node key when it does, and retains both the device signature and the node countersignature in `audit_events.signature_metadata_json` (see the Section 10.1/10.4 row). M12.4 adds the accept-time half of device revocation: an operation naming an acting device this node does not hold, or holds as revoked, is refused before it is stored and is audited as `node_operation.rejected` (see the Section 10.1/10.4 row). Encryption and signing key generation/storage lifecycles, client-side device signing of operations, device public-key registration, and God-mode device revocation UI remain deferred to their owning tasks. The readiness UI surface that displays these signals is delivered by M8.5 (Technical spec Section 14 row). |
